#### Cousin's lemma: fine tagged partitions of a box

**Motivation.** Every gauge statement in the two preceding subsections has the form "every $r$-fine tagged partition satisfies …". If for some gauge $r$ there were no $r$-fine tagged partition at all, such a statement would hold vacuously for every candidate value: every function would have every vector as its integral, and limits along the partition filter would not be unique. Cousin's lemma says that this never happens, whatever the gauge. The formal proof of the divergence theorem uses it at the GP parameter in three places. It makes limits unique, which the proof needs whenever it identifies a box integral with a value computed in another way. It supplies the good partition of each cell in Step 1 of the Henstock–Sacks proof, and the completion of a prepartition in Lemma 1 of that subsection. The gauges in question are arbitrary: the gauge of Hand computation 2 lies between $\varepsilon x/32$ and $\varepsilon x/8$ near the edge $x=0$, so no positive constant bounds it from below. This subsection reads the pinned proof of the lemma, input (N6b) of the preceding subsection. The proof is a bisection argument that uses the completeness of $\mathbb R$ at a single limit point. We also record which conditions on tags, closures, endpoints and distortion each consumer needs. Nothing below uses the Henstock–Sacks inequality or a result proved from it.

**Example.** On $[0,1]$ let $r(0)=1/8$ and $r(x)=x/4$ for $0<x\le1$. This gauge is positive at every point of a compact interval, but its infimum is $0$, and the infimum is not attained. A closed cell $[a,b]$ with a tag $t\in[a,b]$ is $r$-fine when $[a,b]\subseteq[t-r(t),t+r(t)]$. For $t>0$ this means $3t/4\le a$ and $b\le5t/4$. Some $t\in[a,b]$ satisfies both exactly when $a>0$ and $b\le5a/3$. A cell $[0,b]$ must therefore be tagged at $0$, and it is then fine exactly when $b\le1/8$. In a partition of $[0,1]$ into $n\ge2$ cells of equal length $h$, the cell $[h,2h]$ has $b=2a>5a/3$, so it is not fine for any tag in it. For $n=1$ the cell $[0,1]$ is not fine either. Hence no partition into cells of equal length, with tags in their own cells, is $r$-fine, however small the mesh. A fine partition exists nonetheless, but it has to follow the gauge. Hand computation 6 below constructs one with seven cells. Compare a gauge with $r\ge\rho_0>0$ on $[0,1]$. For it, every partition of mesh at most $\rho_0$ is fine for every choice of tags in the cells, because a closed cell of length at most $\rho_0$ lies in the closed ball of radius $\rho_0$ about each of its points. Cousin's lemma has content exactly for gauges without a positive lower bound.

**Definitions.** We use the half-open setting of the pinned library described in the preceding subsection, in $\mathbb R^n$ with the maximum norm. The chapter needs $n=2$ for rectangles and $n=1$ for their faces. A *box* is $J=\prod_{i=1}^n(l_i,u_i]$ with $l_i<u_i$ for every $i$. Its closure $\bar J=\prod_i[l_i,u_i]$ is the closed hull of the preceding subsection. A *tagged partition* of a box $I$ is a finite family of pairwise disjoint boxes contained in $I$ whose union is $I$, with a tag $p_K\in\bar I$ for each cell $K$. It is *Henstock* if $p_K\in\bar K$ for every cell, and *$r$-fine* if, for every cell, $\bar K$ lies in the closed ball of radius $r(p_K)$ about $p_K$. A gauge is a function $r:\mathbb R^n\to(0,\infty)$, and only its values on $\bar I$ matter. We write $\kappa(J)$ for the distortion of a box and $\kappa(\pi)$ for the distortion of a prepartition, as defined there, and we use the admissibility conditions (A1)–(A4).

- *Centre split.* Let $J$ have centre $m=(l+u)/2$. For a set $s$ of coordinates, let $J_s$ be the box whose $i$-th factor is $(m_i,u_i]$ for $i\in s$ and $(l_i,m_i]$ for $i\notin s$. The $2^n$ boxes $J_s$ are the *children* of $J$.
- *Dyadic homothety.* A box $K$ is *homothetic to $J$ with ratio $2^{-k}$* if $u_i(K)-l_i(K)=2^{-k}\bigl(u_i(J)-l_i(J)\bigr)$ for every $i$. Its position is arbitrary.
- *Cousin partition.* For a gauge $r$, an *$r$-Cousin partition* of $J$ is a Henstock, $r$-fine tagged partition of $J$ whose cells are homothetic to $J$ with dyadic ratios and whose distortion equals $\kappa(J)$.

**Lemma 1 (centre split).** [Lean, read] Let $J$ be a box and $s$ a set of coordinates.

- (a) $J_s$ is a box: $l_i<m_i<u_i$ for every $i$.
- (b) A point $y$ lies in $J_s$ if and only if $y\in J$ and, for every $i$, $m_i<y_i$ holds exactly when $i\in s$.
- (c) The children of $J$ are pairwise disjoint, contained in $J$, and their union is $J$. Distinct sets $s$ give distinct children.
- (d) Each side of $J_s$ is half the corresponding side of $J$, so $J_s$ is homothetic to $J$ with ratio $1/2$.

*Proof.* (a) The midpoint of $l_i<u_i$ lies strictly between them. (b) For $i\in s$, $y_i\in(m_i,u_i]$ if and only if $y_i\in(l_i,u_i]$ and $m_i<y_i$, because $l_i<m_i$. For $i\notin s$, $y_i\in(l_i,m_i]$ if and only if $y_i\in(l_i,u_i]$ and $y_i\le m_i$, because $m_i<u_i$. (c) If $y\in J_s\cap J_t$, then by (b) $i\in s$ exactly when $m_i<y_i$, which happens exactly when $i\in t$. So $s=t$. Each child is nonempty, so distinct sets give distinct children. A point $y\in J$ lies in $J_s$ for $s=\{i:m_i<y_i\}$. (d) $u_i-m_i=m_i-l_i=(u_i-l_i)/2$. $\square$ [Centre split](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Box/SubboxInduction.lean#L43-L97) · [As a partition](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/SubboxInduction.lean#L44-L64)

The half-open convention is what makes (c) exact. Closed halves would share the hyperplanes $x_i=m_i$.

**Lemma 2 (induction on subboxes).** [Lean, read] Let $P$ be a property of boxes and $I$ a box. Suppose the following.

- (H1) For every box $J\subseteq I$: if $P$ holds for all children of $J$, then $P(J)$.
- (H2) For every $z\in\bar I$ there is a neighbourhood $U$ of $z$ relative to $\bar I$ with the following property. $P(J)$ holds for every box $J\subseteq I$ that is homothetic to $I$ with a dyadic ratio and satisfies $z\in\bar J\subseteq U$.

Then $P(I)$.

*Proof.* Suppose that $P(I)$ fails. By (H1), every box $J\subseteq I$ for which $P$ fails has a child for which $P$ fails; choose one such child for each such $J$. Start from $J^{(0)}=I$ and pass each time to the chosen child. This gives boxes $I=J^{(0)}\supseteq J^{(1)}\supseteq\cdots$ for which $P$ fails, and by Lemma 1(d) the box $J^{(k)}$ is homothetic to $I$ with ratio $2^{-k}$. Let $l^{(k)}$ and $u^{(k)}$ be the corners of $J^{(k)}$. In every coordinate the lower corners increase, the upper corners decrease, and $l^{(k)}\le u^{(j)}$ for all $j,k$. Let $z$ be the coordinatewise supremum of the lower corners, which exists because $\mathbb R$ is complete. Then $l^{(k)}\le z\le u^{(k)}$ for every $k$, so $z\in\overline{J^{(k)}}$ for every $k$; in particular $z\in\bar I$. The lower corners converge to $z$, being monotone with supremum $z$. Since $u^{(k)}-l^{(k)}=2^{-k}\bigl(u^{(0)}-l^{(0)}\bigr)\to0$, the upper corners converge to $z$ as well. A point $x\in\overline{J^{(k)}}$ satisfies $l^{(k)}\le x\le u^{(k)}$ coordinatewise. Hence $\|x-z\|_\infty\le\max\bigl(\|l^{(k)}-z\|_\infty,\|u^{(k)}-z\|_\infty\bigr)$, and the right side tends to $0$. Now take $U$ from (H2) at the point $z$. It contains $\bar I\cap\bar B(z,\eta)$ for some $\eta>0$, and $\overline{J^{(k)}}\subseteq\bar I$. So $z\in\overline{J^{(k)}}\subseteq U$ for all large $k$, and (H2) gives $P(J^{(k)})$, a contradiction. $\square$ [Induction principle](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Box/SubboxInduction.lean#L99-L161) · [Form with the centre-split partition](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/SubboxInduction.lean#L72-L92)

The pinned proof takes the same steps. A choice function picks the failing children, and $J^{(k)}$ is the $k$-th iterate. The point $z$ comes from the nested-interval property of the coordinatewise order, and the corners converge by monotone convergence and $2^{-k}\to0$. The last step uses the fact that closed boxes whose corners tend to $z$ inside $\bar I$ eventually lie in every neighbourhood of $z$ relative to $\bar I$. The argument is by contradiction and constructs nothing. The limit point lies in every closed box $\overline{J^{(k)}}$ but need not lie in any $J^{(k)}$: the boxes $(0,2^{-k}]$ shrink to $0$, which belongs to none of them. This is why (H2) is stated for $z\in\bar I$, and why tags are only required to lie in closed cells.

**Theorem (Cousin's lemma in a box).** [Lean, read], over the general facts named at the end of this subsection. Let $I$ be a box in $\mathbb R^n$ and $r:\mathbb R^n\to(0,\infty)$ any function. Then $I$ has an $r$-Cousin partition $\pi$, that is, a tagged partition with the following five properties.

1. The cells of $\pi$ are pairwise disjoint boxes with union exactly $I$.
2. Each tag lies in the closure of its own cell.
3. The closure of each cell lies in the closed ball of radius $r(p)$ about its tag $p$.
4. Each cell is homothetic to $I$ with ratio $2^{-k}$, for some $k\ge0$ depending on the cell.
5. The distortion of $\pi$ equals that of $I$: $\kappa(\pi)=\kappa(I)$.

No continuity, measurability or positive lower bound of $r$ is assumed.

*Proof.* Apply Lemma 2 to the property "$J$ has an $r$-Cousin partition", where properties 4 and 5 refer to $J$ in place of $I$.

(H2) Let $z\in\bar I$ and put $U=\bar I\cap\bar B(z,r(z))$, a neighbourhood of $z$ relative to $\bar I$ because $r(z)>0$. Let $J\subseteq I$ be a box with $z\in\bar J\subseteq U$. The single cell $J$ with tag $z$ is an $r$-Cousin partition of $J$. It is Henstock because $z\in\bar J$, and $r$-fine because $\bar J\subseteq\bar B(z,r(z))$. Its cell is homothetic to $J$ with ratio $2^0$, and its distortion is $\kappa(J)$. The dyadic homothety assumed in (H2) is not needed here.

(H1) Let $J\subseteq I$ be a box, and for each child $J_s$ let $\pi_s$ be an $r$-Cousin partition of $J_s$. Let $\pi$ consist of all cells of all $\pi_s$, each with its tag from $\pi_s$. By Lemma 1(c) and property 1 for each $\pi_s$, the cells of $\pi$ are pairwise disjoint with union $J$. Properties 2 and 3 concern one cell and its tag at a time, so they pass to $\pi$. A cell $K$ of $\pi_s$ is homothetic to $J_s$ with some ratio $2^{-k}$, and $J_s$ is homothetic to $J$ with ratio $1/2$ by Lemma 1(d). So $K$ is homothetic to $J$ with ratio $2^{-k-1}$. Multiplying all sides of a box by one positive factor does not change the ratio of its longest side to its shortest side, so every cell of $\pi$ has distortion $\kappa(J)$. Since $\pi$ has at least one cell, its distortion, the maximum over its cells, is $\kappa(J)$. Lemma 2 now gives the theorem. $\square$ [Cousin's lemma](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/SubboxInduction.lean#L94-L131) · [Gluing tagged partitions](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Tagged.lean#L111-L150) · [Single cells](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Tagged.lean#L253-L290) · [Distortion of a box](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Box/Basic.lean#L436-L457)

The pinned proof discards the distortion clause of the children and recomputes property 5 from property 4. Property 4 is the means; property 5 is what the GP parameter consumes.

**Corollary (prepartitions, admissible partitions, nontrivial filters).** [Lean, read], with the complement prepartition of (N6c) used as stated in part (b). Let $\pi_0$ be a prepartition of a box $I$ and let $r$ be a gauge.

- (a) There is a Henstock, $r$-fine tagged prepartition $\pi$ of $I$ whose cells each lie in a cell of $\pi_0$, with $\bigcup\pi=\bigcup\pi_0$ and $\kappa(\pi)=\kappa(\pi_0)$.
- (b) Let $\ell$ be a parameter set and $c\ge0$, and let $\pi_0^c$ be the complement prepartition of $\pi_0$, whose union is $I\setminus\bigcup\pi_0$. If $\kappa(\pi_0)\le c$ and $\kappa(\pi_0^c)\le c$, then the prepartition $\pi$ of (a) is $(c,r)$-admissible for $\ell$, with (A4) witnessed by $\pi_0^c$. For the one-cell prepartition $\{I\}$ the complement is empty. So every box $I$ has a $(c,r)$-admissible tagged partition for every gauge $r$ and every $c\ge\kappa(I)$.
- (c) For every parameter set, the filter of admissible tagged prepartitions with union $\bigcup\pi_0$ is nontrivial. Its version for $\pi_0=\{I\}$ defines the box integral, so a function has at most one box integral on $I$ for each parameter set.

*Proof.* (a) Apply the theorem to each cell of $\pi_0$ with the gauge $r$, and take all cells of the resulting partitions with their tags. Each piece partitions its cell, so the union is $\bigcup\pi_0$. The distortion is the maximum, over the cells $J$ of $\pi_0$, of the distortion of the partition of $J$. By property 5 this is the maximum of the $\kappa(J)$, which is $\kappa(\pi_0)$. The homothety clause is not kept. (b) Conditions (A1) and (A2) hold by (a). (A3) holds because $\kappa(\pi)=\kappa(\pi_0)\le c$. For (A4), the union of $\pi_0^c$ is $I\setminus\bigcup\pi_0=I\setminus\bigcup\pi$, and $\kappa(\pi_0^c)\le c$. For $\pi_0=\{I\}$ the library's complement is the empty prepartition, whose distortion is $0$. (c) Call a gauge *allowed* if it is constant in case the Riemann flag is set; otherwise every gauge is allowed. For a level $c$, the filter at level $c$ has a basis consisting of the sets of $(c,r)$-admissible prepartitions with union $\bigcup\pi_0$, one set for each allowed gauge $r$. That these sets form a basis uses only that the pointwise minimum of two allowed gauges is allowed and that decreasing the gauge preserves admissibility. A filter with a basis is nontrivial as soon as every basis set is nonempty. At the level $c=\max\bigl(\kappa(\pi_0),\kappa(\pi_0^c)\bigr)$ this is (b). The filter used for integration is the supremum of these filters over all $c$, so it is nontrivial as well. Limits along a nontrivial filter are unique. $\square$ [Prepartitions](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/SubboxInduction.lean#L139-L157) · [Admissible partitions](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L482-L493) · [Empty complement](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Split.lean#L336-L357) · [Basis](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L404-L447) · [Nontrivial filters](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L495-L515) · [Uniqueness](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L249-L256)

**Use in the formal proof.** [Lean, read] The planar theorem of this chapter reaches the box integral only through the Bochner divergence theorem, at the GP parameter. All admissibility clauses are then active.

1. *Degenerate endpoints are removed before any box is formed.* The Bochner theorem receives corners $a\le b$. If $a_i=b_i$ for some $i$, the half-open product $\prod_j(a_j,b_j]$ is empty. Then the area integral is $0$, the two face integrals in direction $i$ coincide, and the remaining faces are null. No box is formed and no gauge integral is taken. Otherwise $a_i<b_i$ for every $i$, the box with these corners is formed, and its interior is exhausted by an increasing sequence of boxes $J_k$, to which the GP argument is applied. Cousin's lemma is therefore applied only to nondegenerate boxes. The faces enter as boxes of one dimension less, built from the remaining coordinates, so they are nondegenerate as well.
2. *Uniqueness.* On each $J_k$ the proof obtains two GP integrals of the divergence, one from the Bochner comparison and one from the gauge divergence theorem, and equates them by uniqueness. It identifies the face integrals in the same way. The comparison proof also uses uniqueness to equate the box integral of a simple function with its Bochner integral, which is fact 3 in the return to the comparison proof in the preceding subsection. Uniqueness is part (c) of the corollary. At the GP parameter its nontriviality comes from part (b) for the one-cell prepartition of the box in question, at the level equal to the distortion of that box. In the plane, the faces are intervals of distortion $1$.
3. *Henstock–Sacks, Step 1.* For a cell $J$ of $\pi_0$ the proof notes $\kappa(J)\le C=\max\bigl(\kappa(\pi_0),\kappa(\pi_0^c)\bigr)$ and calls part (b) for the box $J$ at level $C$.
4. *Henstock–Sacks, Lemma 1.* The completion $\pi^r$ is part (a) applied to a complement prepartition whose distortion is at most the relevant level by (A4). The completion has exactly the distortion of that prepartition, so the completed partition still satisfies (A3).

Each consumer needs the same condition: the level $c$ must be at least the distortion of the boxes being subdivided. Dyadic homothety makes subdivision preserve distortion. Bisecting only some of the coordinates would not. Halving only the horizontal sides of a unit square gives cells of distortion $2$, which are not admissible at level $1$. [Degenerate case](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DivergenceTheorem.lean#L266-L291) · [Exhaustion](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DivergenceTheorem.lean#L141-L164) · [Uniqueness calls](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DivergenceTheorem.lean#L108-L136) · [Simple functions](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Integrability.lean#L169-L189) · [Step 1](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L533-L576) · [Completion](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L450-L468) · [Faces](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Box/Basic.lean#L354-L358)

**Hand computation 6 (one dimension, no positive lower bound).** Take $I=(0,1]$ and the gauge of the example, $r(0)=1/8$ and $r(x)=x/4$ for $0<x\le1$. By the example, a cell $(a,b]$ with $a>0$ has an admissible tag exactly when $b\le5a/3$, and then $t=\min(b,4a/3)$ works. A cell $(0,b]$ needs the tag $0$ and $b\le1/8$. Run the recursion suggested by Lemma 2: keep a cell that has an admissible tag, and otherwise replace it by its two halves.

- The cells $(0,1]$, $(0,1/2]$ and $(0,1/4]$ contain $0$ in their closures and are longer than $1/8$, so they are split. The cell $(0,1/8]$ is kept with tag $0$.
- The cells $(1/2,1]$, $(1/4,1/2]$ and $(1/8,1/4]$ have $b=2a>5a/3$ and are split. Their halves have $b/a=3/2$ or $4/3$ and are kept with tag $4a/3$.

| Cell | Closure | Length | Tag $t$ | $r(t)$ | Closed ball |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $(0,1/8]$ | $[0,1/8]$ | $2^{-3}$ | $0$ | $1/8$ | $[-1/8,1/8]$ |
| $(1/8,3/16]$ | $[1/8,3/16]$ | $2^{-4}$ | $1/6$ | $1/24$ | $[1/8,5/24]$ |
| $(3/16,1/4]$ | $[3/16,1/4]$ | $2^{-4}$ | $1/4$ | $1/16$ | $[3/16,5/16]$ |
| $(1/4,3/8]$ | $[1/4,3/8]$ | $2^{-3}$ | $1/3$ | $1/12$ | $[1/4,5/12]$ |
| $(3/8,1/2]$ | $[3/8,1/2]$ | $2^{-3}$ | $1/2$ | $1/8$ | $[3/8,5/8]$ |
| $(1/2,3/4]$ | $[1/2,3/4]$ | $2^{-2}$ | $2/3$ | $1/6$ | $[1/2,5/6]$ |
| $(3/4,1]$ | $[3/4,1]$ | $2^{-2}$ | $1$ | $1/4$ | $[3/4,5/4]$ |

*Fineness, cell by cell.* Every tag lies in its closed cell. The tags $1/6\in[1/8,3/16]$, $1/3\in[1/4,3/8]$ and $2/3\in[1/2,3/4]$ are interior points, and the others are endpoints. For the six cells with $a>0$, the left end of the ball is $3t/4=a$ exactly. The right end is $5t/4=5a/3$, which is at least $b$: $5/24>3/16$, $5/16>1/4$, $5/12>3/8$, $5/8>1/2$, $5/6>3/4$ and $5/4>1$. The first cell lies in $[-1/8,1/8]$, with equality at its right end. The lengths $2^{-3},2^{-4},2^{-4},2^{-3},2^{-3},2^{-2},2^{-2}$ add up to $1$, and consecutive cells share endpoints, so the seven half-open cells partition $(0,1]$. Every cell is homothetic to $I$ with a dyadic ratio. In one dimension every box has distortion $1$, so property 5 holds trivially. The tag $0$ of the first cell is not a point of the half-open cell $(0,1/8]$. The Henstock condition refers to closures, and this is where that matters.

*Why the recursion stops, and what compactness gives.* Every split cell either contains $0$ in its closure or has $b=2a$. The cells of the second kind stop after one split. The cells of the first kind form the nested sequence $(0,2^{-k}]$, which shrinks to $z=0$, and it stops at the first cell that fits into $\bar B(0,r(0))$. The values of $r$ on $(0,1/8)$, which are arbitrarily small, are never consulted: the cell next to $0$ is fine because of $r(0)=1/8$ alone, the value at the limit point that Lemma 2 uses. With $r(0)=2^{-K}$ for an integer $K\ge0$ instead, the same recursion gives $2K+1$ cells. The radii at the seven tags have minimum $1/24>0$, yet by the example the uniform partition of mesh $1/24$ is not $r$-fine. A fine partition needs a positive minimum only over its finitely many tags, and that minimum says nothing about the gauge between them.

**Hand computation 7 (two dimensions, a nonconstant gauge, distortion 2).** Let $I=(0,2]\times(0,1]$, so $\bar I=[0,2]\times[0,1]$ and $\kappa(I)=2$. Let $r(p)=\|p\|_\infty/2$ for $p\ne0$ and $r(0)=1/4$. The gauge tends to $0$ at the corner $0$ and equals $1/4$ there. Two observations decide every cell.

- (i) If the closure of a cell contains $0$, its only possible tag is $0$. A tag $p\ne0$ is at distance $\|p\|_\infty>r(p)$ from the point $0$ of the cell. With the tag $0$, the cell is fine exactly when both of its sides are at most $1/4$.
- (ii) For $\lambda>0$ and $p\ne0$, $r(\lambda p)=\lambda r(p)$. Scaling by $\lambda$ a cell whose closure avoids $0$, together with its tag, therefore preserves fineness.

Let $C_k=(0,2^{1-k}]\times(0,2^{-k}]$ be the corner cell of generation $k$, so that $C_0=I$. The other three children of $C_{k-1}$ are $A_k=(2^{1-k},2^{2-k}]\times(0,2^{-k}]$, $B_k=(0,2^{1-k}]\times(2^{-k},2^{1-k}]$ and $D_k=(2^{1-k},2^{2-k}]\times(2^{-k},2^{1-k}]$, and they are $A_1$, $B_1$, $D_1$ scaled by $2^{1-k}$. By (i), $C_k$ is fine with tag $0$ exactly when $2^{1-k}\le1/4$, that is, when $k\ge3$. So $C_0$, $C_1$ and $C_2$ are split and $C_3$ is kept. The closures of $A_1$, $B_1$ and $D_1$ avoid $0$, and the table shows that they are fine with the tags $(2,\tfrac12)$, $(\tfrac12,1)$ and $(2,1)$. By (ii), $A_k$, $B_k$ and $D_k$ are fine with these tags multiplied by $2^{1-k}$. The recursion therefore returns ten cells.

| Cell | Closure | Tag $z$ | $r(z)$ | Closed ball |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $A_1$ | $[1,2]\times[0,\tfrac12]$ | $(2,\tfrac12)$ | $1$ | $[1,3]\times[-\tfrac12,\tfrac32]$ |
| $B_1$ | $[0,1]\times[\tfrac12,1]$ | $(\tfrac12,1)$ | $\tfrac12$ | $[0,1]\times[\tfrac12,\tfrac32]$ |
| $D_1$ | $[1,2]\times[\tfrac12,1]$ | $(2,1)$ | $1$ | $[1,3]\times[0,2]$ |
| $A_2$ | $[\tfrac12,1]\times[0,\tfrac14]$ | $(1,\tfrac14)$ | $\tfrac12$ | $[\tfrac12,\tfrac32]\times[-\tfrac14,\tfrac34]$ |
| $B_2$ | $[0,\tfrac12]\times[\tfrac14,\tfrac12]$ | $(\tfrac14,\tfrac12)$ | $\tfrac14$ | $[0,\tfrac12]\times[\tfrac14,\tfrac34]$ |
| $D_2$ | $[\tfrac12,1]\times[\tfrac14,\tfrac12]$ | $(1,\tfrac12)$ | $\tfrac12$ | $[\tfrac12,\tfrac32]\times[0,1]$ |
| $A_3$ | $[\tfrac14,\tfrac12]\times[0,\tfrac18]$ | $(\tfrac12,\tfrac18)$ | $\tfrac14$ | $[\tfrac14,\tfrac34]\times[-\tfrac18,\tfrac38]$ |
| $B_3$ | $[0,\tfrac14]\times[\tfrac18,\tfrac14]$ | $(\tfrac18,\tfrac14)$ | $\tfrac18$ | $[0,\tfrac14]\times[\tfrac18,\tfrac38]$ |
| $D_3$ | $[\tfrac14,\tfrac12]\times[\tfrac18,\tfrac14]$ | $(\tfrac12,\tfrac14)$ | $\tfrac14$ | $[\tfrac14,\tfrac34]\times[0,\tfrac12]$ |
| $C_3$ | $[0,\tfrac14]\times[0,\tfrac18]$ | $(0,0)$ | $\tfrac14$ | $[-\tfrac14,\tfrac14]\times[-\tfrac14,\tfrac14]$ |

*Fineness, cell by cell.* In each row the closed cell lies in the closed ball, as one sees by comparing the four endpoints. Each tag lies in its closed cell. The tags of $A_k$, $D_k$ and $C_3$ are corners of their cells, and the tag of $B_k$ is the midpoint of its upper side. The areas add up to $3\cdot\tfrac12+3\cdot\tfrac18+3\cdot\tfrac1{32}+\tfrac1{32}=2$. The ten half-open cells arise by repeated centre splits, so by Lemma 1(c) they partition $I$. A cell of generation $k$ has sides $2^{1-k}$ and $2^{-k}$, so it is homothetic to $I$ with ratio $2^{-k}$ and has distortion $2$. The partition is therefore $(c,r)$-admissible for the GP parameter at every level $c\ge2$, which includes the level $\kappa(I)$ used by the corollary.

*Where closedness is used.* The cell $B_1$ has exactly one admissible tag. A tag $z\in[0,1]\times[\tfrac12,1]$ has $r(z)=\max(z_1,z_2)/2\le\tfrac12$. Covering the horizontal side $[0,1]$ needs $r(z)\ge\max(z_1,1-z_1)\ge\tfrac12$. Hence $r(z)=\tfrac12$, so $z_1=\tfrac12$ and $\max(z_1,z_2)=1$, that is, $z=(\tfrac12,1)$. With this tag the ball meets the left, right and lower sides of the cell exactly. If fineness required the closed cell to lie in the open ball, this tag would be excluded and $B_1$ would have to be split, and likewise each $B_k$ and $C_3$. The partitions depend on the convention, but existence does not: the proof works equally with $U=\bar I\cap B(z,r(z))$.

*Where the distortion clause is used.* The right half $(1,2]\times(0,1]$ can also be cut into $k\ge2$ vertical strips $(1+\tfrac{j-1}k,1+\tfrac jk]\times(0,1]$ with tags $(1+\tfrac jk,\tfrac12)$. There $r=(1+j/k)/2>\tfrac12$, so the ball covers the height $1$ and the width $1/k$ of the strip, and the strips are $r$-fine Henstock cells. Together with $B_1$ and the seven cells that partition $C_1$, they form an $r$-fine Henstock partition of $I$ of distortion $k$. It is admissible for the Henstock and McShane parameters, but not for the GP parameter at any level $c<k$. The cells of the theorem never exceed the distortion of $I$, whatever the gauge.

**Remarks.**

1. *Compactness is not a lower bound.* [Expository] The closed box is compact, and Lemma 2 is in effect the bisection proof of that compactness. It uses the completeness of $\mathbb R$ through the supremum of the lower corners. What it extracts from the gauge is only its positivity at one point, the limit of a failing nested sequence. A positive function on a compact set has a positive minimum when it is lower semicontinuous, but not in general. The gauge of Hand computation 6 is not lower semicontinuous at $0$, since $r(x)\to0<r(0)$ as $x\to0^+$. The gauge of Hand computation 2 behaves in the same way at each point of the edge $x=0$, where it equals $\varepsilon2^{-2N_0-3}$ while its values tend to $0$ as $x\to0^+$. Kurzweil's proof of the one-dimensional lemma uses compactness in the covering form [Kurzweil1957, p. 423] [Paper]. It extracts a minimal finite family of the open intervals $(\tau-\delta(\tau),\tau+\delta(\tau))$ that still covers the interval, orders their centres, and places a division point in the overlap of each two consecutive intervals, between their centres. The tags are those centres, and their finitely many radii have a positive minimum. That minimum bounds the gauge only at the chosen centres, which is the point of Hand computation 6. In reverse mathematics the strength of Cousin's lemma grows with the complexity of the gauge. Barrett, Downey and Greenberg show that over $\mathsf{RCA}_0$ the lemma is equivalent to $\mathsf{WKL}_0$ for continuous gauges, to $\mathsf{ACA}_0$ for Baire class 1 gauges, and to $\mathsf{ATR}_0$ for Baire class 2 gauges and for Borel gauges, the last two modulo some induction [BarrettDowneyGreenberg2022] [Paper].
2. *Endpoints and degenerate boxes.* [Lean, read] Boxes are nondegenerate by definition, and centre splitting keeps them so by Lemma 1(a). A product with some $l_i=u_i$ is empty in the half-open convention and is not a box. The divergence theorem removes such rectangles before any gauge integral is formed, as described above, so Cousin's lemma never has to partition one. Tags may sit at endpoints and corners of their cells, even at a corner that does not belong to the half-open cell, as at $0$ in both hand computations, because the Henstock condition and fineness only see the closed cell.
3. *A terminating recursion.* [Expository] The pinned proof is by contradiction and selects children by choice, so it computes no partition. Hand computations 6 and 7 used the recursion "keep a cell that has an admissible tag, otherwise split it into its children". This recursion stops for every gauge. To see this, apply Lemma 2 to the property "the recursion started at $J$ stops". (H1) holds because a split cell stops once all its children stop. (H2) holds with the neighbourhood $U$ of the theorem's proof, because a cell $J$ with $z\in\bar J\subseteq U$ is kept at once with the tag $z$. The rectangular divergence proof above sketched the same termination argument through an infinite subdivision tree, which by finite branching has an infinite nested branch. The pinned proof replaces the tree by the choice of one failing child at each step.
4. *Sources and reading level.* Cousin's Lemma 10 [Cousin1895, p. 22] concerns a connected plane region $S$ bounded by a closed contour, simple or complex, with a circle of nonzero radius about each point of $S$ or of its perimeter. It asserts that $S$ can be subdivided into finitely many regions, each completely interior to the circle of a suitably chosen point of $S$ or of its perimeter [Paper]. By his §9 on the same page, "completely interior" means that every point of the region and of its perimeter is interior to the circle, so his regions are closed regions inside open discs, the open-ball convention of Hand computation 7 in the Euclidean norm. His proof divides $S$ into squares and parts of squares by parallels to the axes and keeps a piece for which the lemma fails. Iteration gives pieces $S_1,S_2,\dots$ that tend to a point $M$ of $S$ or of its perimeter. Arbitrarily small $S_p$ surround $M$ or adjoin it, yet none lies inside a circle of the statement, which is impossible because $M$ has a circle of nonzero radius. This is Lemma 2 in the plane, with squares and parts of squares in place of the $2^n$ children. The statement lets the chosen point lie anywhere in $S$ or on its perimeter, but the contradiction uses the circle of a point $M$ in or adjoining the small square, which is the Henstock form [Expository]. Maurey and Tacchi quote the lemma and its proof in their history of the covering theorem [MaureyTacchi2005, pp. 172–173]. Kurzweil's existence lemma for gauge subdivisions of an interval is Lemma 1,1,1 of [Kurzweil1957, p. 423]. Barrett, Downey and Greenberg describe the role of the lemma this way: without it, "Kurzweil's definition of the gauge integral would be vacuously satisfied by all functions and all values" [BarrettDowneyGreenberg2022, §1]. Both pinned files are by Yury Kudryashov. The exposition, the two hand computations, the tracing of the GP calls and the termination argument of Remark 3 are ours. The general facts named after the table are [Lean]. Part (c) of the corollary proves the clauses of (N6a) on nontriviality and uniqueness; the $\varepsilon$–gauge description in (N6a) remains [Lean], as does (N6c), which part (b) uses as stated. The theorem uses none of (N6a), (N6c) and (N6d), and it does not use the Henstock–Sacks inequality.

#### Where interval lengths in real Lebesgue measure come from

**Motivation.** Every one-dimensional integral in this chapter ultimately spends three properties of real Lebesgue measure $\lambda$ (`volume : Measure ℝ`): a bounded interval with ordered endpoints $a \le b$ has finite measure $b - a$, singletons carry mass zero so open, half-open and closed intervals have the same integral, and every real point has an open neighborhood of finite measure so local filter estimates apply. In Lemma 3.3 (`windowHyp_of_rpow`) below, passing from the closed window $[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]$ to the oriented integral $\int_Y^{Y^{1+\delta}}$ rewrites $\int_{[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]}$ to $\int_{(Y, Y^{1+\delta}]}$ by deleting the single left endpoint $Y$. In the fundamental theorem of calculus for oriented interval integrals—used on horizontal wedge slices to build holomorphic primitives on disks and on circle arcs in the archive's winding-number calculation—local finiteness supplies an integrable neighborhood at the moving endpoint, while the constant-integral formula $\int_u^v c\,dx = (v - u)\cdot c$ and the norm bound $\bigl\lVert\int_u^v f(x)\,dx\bigr\rVert \le C\lvert v - u\rvert$ turn measure-theoretic small-set errors into derivatives with respect to real length.

Where do those interval lengths actually come from in the pinned library, and why can an oriented integral be negative when every set has nonnegative measure? Perhaps surprisingly, Mathlib does **not** define `(volume : Measure ℝ)` directly by interval differences: $\mathbb R$ inherits `volume` as a one-dimensional real inner product space, namely the additive Haar measure normalized on the unit parallelepiped of `stdOrthonormalBasis ℝ ℝ`. Separately, Mathlib associates a Borel Stieltjes measure to any monotone right-continuous function $f : R \to \mathbb R$ by feeding the single-interval envelope cost into Carathéodory's outer-measure construction, evaluates it on $(a, b]$ by two right-continuity shifts and Heine–Borel compactness, and bridges the two constructions at `Real.volume_eq_stieltjes_id` by applying Haar uniqueness to the identity function $x \mapsto x$. Opening this chain discharges the real-volume boundary left open by the local filter-integrability and endpoint-FTC packages without assuming any interval formula in advance.

**Hand calculation 1 (oriented intervals, half-open partitions and integral signs).** [Expository] Give $E = \mathbb R^2$ the norm $\lVert(u, v)\rVert = \lvert u\rvert + \lvert v\rvert$, equip $(\mathbb R, \mathcal B(\mathbb R))$ with Lebesgue measure $\lambda$, and take breakpoints $-1 < \frac{1}{2} < 2$. Define $f : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R^2$ by
$$
f(x) = \begin{cases}
(5, 5), & x = -1,\\
(2, -1), & x \in (-1, \tfrac{1}{2}),\\
(10, -7), & x = \tfrac{1}{2},\\
(-4, 3), & x \in (\tfrac{1}{2}, 2],\\
(0, 0), & \text{otherwise.}
\end{cases}
$$
Write $(t)_+ := \max(t, 0)$. Under Theorem 1 below, every half-open interval $(a, b]$ has nonnegative set measure $\lambda((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$ and every singleton has measure zero:
- *Forward versus reversed set measures.* On the ordered partition $(-1, 2] = (-1, \frac{1}{2}] \sqcup (\frac{1}{2}, 2]$, the set measures are $\lambda((-1, \frac{1}{2}]) = \frac{3}{2}$, $\lambda((\frac{1}{2}, 2]) = \frac{3}{2}$, and $\lambda((-1, 2]) = \frac{3}{2} + \frac{3}{2} = 3$. When the endpoints are reversed, $(2, -1] = (\frac{1}{2}, -1] = (2, \frac{1}{2}] = \emptyset$, so $\lambda((2, -1]) = (-1 - 2)_+ = 0$—**never** $-3$. Meanwhile the unordered half-open support $\operatorname{uIoc}(2, -1) := (\min(2, -1), \max(2, -1)] = (-1, 2]$ still has positive measure $\lvert -1 - 2\rvert = 3$.
- *Seam spike, endpoint exclusion and orientation sign.* The left value $f(-1) = (5, 5)$ lies outside $(-1, 2]$, and even on $[-1, 2]$ contributes $(5, 5)\,\lambda(\{-1\}) = (0, 0)$. The interior seam $x = \frac{1}{2}$ belongs to $(-1, \frac{1}{2}]$ and not to $(\frac{1}{2}, 2]$, yet the spike $f(\frac{1}{2}) = (10, -7)$ lies on $\{\frac{1}{2}\}$ of measure zero, so
$$
\int_{(-1, 1/2]} f\,d\lambda = \frac{3}{2}(2, -1) = \bigl(3, -\tfrac{3}{2}\bigr), \qquad
\int_{(1/2, 2]} f\,d\lambda = \frac{3}{2}(-4, 3) = \bigl(-6, \tfrac{9}{2}\bigr),
$$
and $\int_{(-1, 2]} f\,d\lambda = (-3, 3)$ while $\int_{(2, -1]} f\,d\lambda = \int_\emptyset f\,d\lambda = (0, 0)$. Mathlib defines the oriented integral by subtracting the two nonnegative-measure half-open integrals:
$$
\int_a^b f(x)\,d\mu := \int_{(a, b]} f\,d\mu - \int_{(b, a]} f\,d\mu.
$$
Thus $\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\lambda = (-3, 3) - (0, 0) = (-3, 3)$, whereas reversing the limits gives
$$
\int_2^{-1} f\,d\lambda = \int_{(2, -1]} f\,d\lambda - \int_{(-1, 2]} f\,d\lambda = (0, 0) - (-3, 3) = (3, -3) = -\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\lambda.
$$
For a constant vector $c = (1, 2)$, $\int_2^{-1} c\,d\lambda = \bigl(\lambda((2, -1]) - \lambda((-1, 2])\bigr)c = (0 - 3)(1, 2) = (-3, -6) = (-1 - 2)c$: the factor $-3$ arises from subtracting two nonnegative set lengths, not from a set of negative measure. Off the null singleton $\{\frac{1}{2}\}$, $\lVert f(x)\rVert$ equals $3$ on $(-1, \frac{1}{2})$ and $7$ on $(\frac{1}{2}, 2]$, so $\lVert f\rVert \le C := 7$ $\lambda$-almost everywhere on $\operatorname{uIoc}(2, -1)$ and
$$
\Bigl\lVert\int_2^{-1} f\,d\lambda\Bigr\rVert = 6 \le \int_{\operatorname{uIoc}(2, -1)} \lVert f\rVert\,d\lambda = \frac{3}{2}\cdot 3 + \frac{3}{2}\cdot 7 = 15 \le 7\,\lvert -1 - 2\rvert = 21.
$$
- *Non-monotone cycle and two-sided endpoint quotients.* Take the non-monotone triple $(-1, 2, \frac{1}{2})$, which overshoots $\frac{1}{2}$ to $2$ and returns to $\frac{1}{2}$. Since $\int_2^{1/2} f\,d\lambda = (0, 0) - (-6, \frac{9}{2}) = (6, -\frac{9}{2})$, adjacent additivity holds with signs:
$$
\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\lambda + \int_2^{1/2} f\,d\lambda = (-3, 3) + \bigl(6, -\tfrac{9}{2}\bigr) = \bigl(3, -\tfrac{3}{2}\bigr) = \int_{-1}^{1/2} f\,d\lambda.
$$
At the interior point $b_0 = 1 \in (\frac{1}{2}, 2)$, $\int_{-1}^1 f\,d\lambda = (3, -\frac{3}{2}) + \frac{1}{2}(-4, 3) = (1, 0)$. For $h = \frac{1}{4}$, $\int_{-1}^{5/4} f\,d\lambda = (0, \frac{3}{4})$ uses $(1, \frac{5}{4}]$ of length $\frac{1}{4}$; for $h = -\frac{1}{4}$, $\int_{-1}^{3/4} f\,d\lambda = (2, -\frac{3}{4})$ subtracts $(\frac{3}{4}, 1]$ of length $\frac{1}{4}$. Both difference quotients $\frac{1}{h}\bigl(\int_{-1}^{1+h} f\,d\lambda - \int_{-1}^1 f\,d\lambda\bigr)$ equal $(-4, 3) = f(1)$.

**Hand calculation 2 (countable open-set decomposition with a countable endpoint set and an accumulation point).** [Expository] For each integer $n \ge 0$, set $a_n := 1 - 2^{-n}$, $b_n := 1 - 3\cdot 2^{-(n+2)}$, and $I_n := (a_n, b_n)$. The first four components are $(0, \frac{1}{4})$, $(\frac{1}{2}, \frac{5}{8})$, $(\frac{3}{4}, \frac{13}{16})$ and $(\frac{7}{8}, \frac{29}{32})$, of lengths $\frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{8}, \frac{1}{16}, \frac{1}{32}$. Because $b_n = 1 - \frac{3}{4}\cdot 2^{-n} < 1 - \frac{1}{2}\cdot 2^{-n} = a_{n+1} < 1$, even the closed intervals $[a_n, b_n]$ are pairwise disjoint, and both $a_n$ and $b_n$ increase strictly to $1$. Let
$$
U := \bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty I_n \subseteq (0, 1).
$$
1. *Single-envelope cost versus countable-cover measure.* Every single half-open interval $(a, b]$ containing $U$ must satisfy $a \le \inf U = 0$ and $b \ge \sup U = 1$, while $U \subseteq (0, 1]$. Thus the raw single-interval envelope cost $\ell(U)$ defined below equals $1 - 0 = 1$. By contrast, each open component $I_n$ is Borel of measure $b_n - a_n = 2^{-(n+2)}$, the partial unions $U_N = \bigsqcup_{n=0}^{N-1} I_n$ have measures $\frac{1}{4}, \frac{3}{8}, \frac{7}{16}, \frac{15}{32}, \dots, \frac{1}{2}(1 - 2^{-N})$, and countable additivity gives
$$
\lambda(U) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \lambda(I_n) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty 2^{-(n+2)} = \frac{1/4}{1 - 1/2} = \frac{1}{2} < 1 = \ell(U).
$$
2. *Countable boundary and open-gap check.* Put $E_0 := \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty \{a_n, b_n\}$ and $E := E_0 \cup \{1\}$. Every point of $E$ lies in $\overline U$ since $a_n, b_n \in \overline{I_n}$ and $1 = \lim_{n\to\infty} b_n$. Conversely, the complement of $U \cup E$ is
$$
\mathbb R \setminus (U \cup E) = (-\infty, 0) \sqcup \Bigl(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (b_n, a_{n+1})\Bigr) \sqcup (1, \infty),
$$
a union of open intervals, so $U \cup E$ is closed; hence $\overline U = U \cup E$ and $\partial U = \overline U \setminus U = E$. Because $E$ is countable and every singleton has $\lambda$-measure zero, $\lambda(E_0) = \lambda(E) = 0$. Consequently, adjoining left or right endpoints or taking the full closure leaves the measure unchanged:
$$
\lambda(U) = \lambda\Bigl(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (a_n, b_n]\Bigr) = \lambda\Bigl(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty [a_n, b_n]\Bigr) = \lambda(\overline U) = \frac{1}{2}.
$$
Meanwhile the open gap union $G := \bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (b_n, a_{n+1})$ inside $(0, 1)$ has gap lengths $a_{n+1} - b_n = 2^{-(n+2)}$ and measure $\lambda(G) = \frac{1}{2}$, matching the disjoint partition $(0, 1] = U \sqcup G \sqcup (E \setminus \{0\})$ of total measure $\frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} + 0 = 1$. Countability of $\partial U$ is essential here: if one instead deletes open middle intervals of total length $\frac{1}{2}$ to form a Smith–Volterra–Cantor open complement $V \subseteq (0, 1)$, the countably many component endpoints still have measure zero, whereas $\partial V = \overline V \setminus V$ is an uncountable nowhere-dense Cantor set of measure $1 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2} > 0$.

**Definitions and named inputs.** Let $\mathbb R$ carry its order topology and Borel $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal B(\mathbb R)$ (the $\sigma$-algebra generated by all open subsets of $\mathbb R$). In `Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Measure/Stieltjes.lean`, a `StieltjesFunction R` bundles a map $f : R \to \mathbb R$ that is monotone ($x \le y \implies f(x) \le f(y)$) and right-continuous at every point ($\lim_{y \to x^+} f(y) = f(x)$) [Lean, read]. On $R = \mathbb R$, which is nonempty and has neither a least element (`botSet = ∅`) nor a greatest element (`Iotop a b = Ioo a b`), the identity `StieltjesFunction.id` is $f(x) = x$, with both right and left limits equal to $x$ (`id_leftLim`) [Lean, read]. Its **single-interval envelope cost** (`StieltjesFunction.length`) is defined on **every** subset $S \subseteq \mathbb R$ by
$$
\ell(S) := \inf\bigl\{ (b - a)_+ \in [0, \infty] : a, b \in \mathbb R,\ S \subseteq (a, b] \bigr\},
$$
with $\inf \emptyset = \infty$ when $S$ is not bounded above and below. [Pinned `Stieltjes.lean:62–169,266–276`][ch3-rv-stieltjes-def]

To avoid circularity, we state explicitly the four general inputs that remain `[Lean]` (or are inherited from Chapter 15's audited sections) and contain **no** real interval-length assumption:

- **(T1) Heine–Borel finite subcover on $[a, b]$.** [Lean] Every covering of a closed bounded real interval $[a, b]$ by open sets admits a finite subcovering (`isCompact_Icc.elim_finite_subcover_image`).
- **(C1) Outer measure from a raw set cost and Carathéodory splitting.** [Lean, read] *(Proved and audited in Chapter 15; `OuterMeasure/OfFunction.lean:57–130`, `OuterMeasure/Caratheodory.lean:55–214`.)* For *any* set $X$ and *any* function $m : \mathcal P(X) \to [0, \infty]$ satisfying $m(\emptyset) = 0$, the countable-cover infimum
$$
\mu^*(S) := \inf\Bigl\{ \sum_{i=0}^\infty m(T_i) : (T_i)_{i \in \mathbb N} \subseteq \mathcal P(X),\ S \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty T_i \Bigr\}
$$
is an outer measure on $X$ ($\mu^*(\emptyset) = 0$, monotone, countably subadditive) satisfying $\mu^*(S) \le m(S)$ for all $S \subseteq X$. The Carathéodory family $\mathcal C(\mu^*) := \{ E \subseteq X : \forall T \subseteq X,\ \mu^*(T) = \mu^*(T \cap E) + \mu^*(T \setminus E) \}$ is a $\sigma$-algebra on which $\mu^*$ is countably additive over pairwise disjoint sequences (`iUnion_eq_of_caratheodory`), and any set $E \subseteq X$ that splits the raw cost subadditively—meaning $m(T \cap E) + m(T \setminus E) \le m(T)$ for all $T \subseteq X$—belongs to $\mathcal C(\mu^*)$ (`ofFunction_caratheodory`). [Pinned `OfFunction.lean`][ch3-rv-offunc] · [Pinned `Caratheodory.lean`][ch3-rv-cara]
- **(U1) Rational open-interval uniqueness for locally finite Borel measures.** [Lean, read] Two Borel measures $\mu,\nu$ on $\mathbb R$, with $\mu$ locally finite, are equal when they agree on $(p,q)$ for every rational pair $p,q$. The finite-restriction proof of this input is given [below](#how-rational-open-intervals-determine-a-real-borel-measure). [Pinned `Real.measure_ext_Ioo_rat`][ch3-rv-borel-real]
- **(U2) Inner-product basis definition of `volume` and $\sigma$-finite Haar uniqueness.** [Lean, read] *(Haar uniqueness proved and audited in [Chapter 15, §15.4.3.3](../chapters/15-two-proofs-side-by-side.md#15433-uniqueness-of-haar-measure-proportionality-normalization-and-the-constant-in-the-half-density-transport); `Haar/OfBasis.lean:97–125,187–202,255–320`, `Haar/Basic.lean:670–686`.)* On $\mathbb R$ as a one-dimensional real inner product space, `(volume : Measure ℝ)` is defined as `b₀.toBasis.addHaar = addHaarMeasure K₀`, where $b_0 = \text{`stdOrthonormalBasis ℝ ℝ`}$ and $K_0 = \text{`parallelepiped b₀`}$ equals either $[0, 1]$ or $[-1, 0]$ (`parallelepiped_orthonormalBasis_one_dim`, since a unit vector in $\mathbb R$ is $\pm 1$). For any $\sigma$-finite left-translation-invariant Borel measure $\mu$ on $\mathbb R$, `addHaarMeasure_unique` gives $\mu = \mu(K_0)\cdot\operatorname{addHaarMeasure}(K_0)$. [Pinned `OfBasis.lean`][ch3-rv-basis] · [Pinned `Haar/Basic.lean`][ch3-rv-haar]

**Lemma 1 (single-interval envelope and compact-to-open peeling).** [Lean, read], conditional on **(T1)**.
1. *Envelope properties (`length_empty`, `length_mono`, `length_Ioc`).* On $\mathbb R$, $\ell(\emptyset) = 0$, $S_1 \subseteq S_2 \implies \ell(S_1) \le \ell(S_2)$, and $\ell((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb R$.
2. *Compact-to-open subadditivity (`length_subadditive_Icc_Ioo`).* For any $a, b \in \mathbb R$ and real sequences $(c_i)_{i \in \mathbb N}, (d_i)_{i \in \mathbb N}$, if $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty (c_i, d_i)$, then $(b - a)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty (d_i - c_i)_+$. [Pinned `Stieltjes.lean:281–307,334–364`][ch3-rv-peel]

**Proof.** For (1), $\emptyset \subseteq (0, 0]$ gives $\ell(\emptyset) \le (0 - 0)_+ = 0$, and monotonicity is immediate from shrinking the set of admissible envelopes. Taking $(a, b]$ itself gives $\ell((a, b]) \le (b - a)_+$. Conversely, suppose $(a, b] \subseteq (a', b']$. If $b \le a$, then $(b - a)_+ = 0 \le (b' - a')_+$. If $a < b$, then $b \in (a, b] \subseteq (a', b']$ forces $b \le b'$; if $a < a'$, setting $x := \min\bigl(\frac{a + a'}{2}, b\bigr)$ would give $x \in (a, b]$ with $x < a'$, contradicting $x \in (a', b']$. Thus $a' \le a < b \le b'$, so $(b - a)_+ = b - a \le b' - a' = (b' - a')_+$. Taking the infimum over $(a', b']$ yields $\ell((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$.

For (2), **(T1)** extracts a finite subset $F \subseteq \mathbb N$ such that $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i \in F} (c_i, d_i)$. Since $\sum_{i \in F} (d_i - c_i)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty (d_i - c_i)_+$, it suffices to prove by strong induction on $\lvert F\rvert$, universally quantified over $b \in \mathbb R$ with $a$ fixed, that $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i \in F} (c_i, d_i)$ implies $(b - a)_+ \le \sum_{i \in F} (d_i - c_i)_+$. If $b \le a$, the left-hand side is $0$. Otherwise $a < b$, so $b \in [a, b]$ lies in $(c_i, d_i)$ for some $i \in F$, meaning $c_i < b < d_i$. Set $F' := F \setminus \{i\}$, so $\lvert F'\rvert = \lvert F\rvert - 1$. Every $x \in [a, c_i]$ satisfies $a \le x \le c_i < b$, hence belongs to $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{j \in F} (c_j, d_j)$, and cannot lie in $(c_i, d_i)$ because $x \le c_i$; thus $[a, c_i] \subseteq \bigcup_{j \in F'} (c_j, d_j)$ (vacuously if $c_i < a$). By the induction hypothesis at $(F', c_i)$, subadditivity $(u + v)_+ \le u_+ + v_+$, and $b < d_i$,
$$
(b - a)_+ \le (c_i - a)_+ + (b - c_i)_+ \le \sum_{j \in F'} (d_j - c_j)_+ + (d_i - c_i)_+ = \sum_{j \in F} (d_j - c_j)_+. \qquad \square
$$

**Lemma 2 (outer-measure recovery, Borel measurability and trimming).** [Lean, read], conditional on **(T1)** and **(C1)**. Let $\mu^*$ be the outer measure built from $\ell$ by **(C1)**.
1. *Exact half-open outer measure (`outer_Ioc`).* For all $a, b \in \mathbb R$, $\mu^*((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$.
2. *Ray splitting and Borel sets (`measurableSet_Ioi`, `borel_le_measurable`).* Every open ray $(c, \infty)$ lies in $\mathcal C(\mu^*)$, and hence $\mathcal B(\mathbb R) \subseteq \mathcal C(\mu^*)$.
3. *No trimming gap (`outer_trim`, `StieltjesFunction.measure`).* For every subset $S \subseteq \mathbb R$, $\inf\{ \mu^*(B) : S \subseteq B,\ B \in \mathcal B(\mathbb R) \} = \mu^*(S)$, so $\mu^*$ directly defines a Borel measure $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} := \text{`StieltjesFunction.id.measure`}$ with $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$. [Pinned `Stieltjes.lean:367–527`][ch3-rv-outer]

**Proof.** (1) By **(C1)** and Lemma 1(1), $\mu^*((a, b]) \le \ell((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$. For the lower bound, if $b \le a$ then $(b - a)_+ = 0 \le \mu^*((a, b])$. Assume $a < b$, let $(a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty S_i$, and suppose $\sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(S_i) < \infty$ (otherwise the bound is trivial). Given $\varepsilon > 0$, set $\delta := \varepsilon / 2 > 0$ and $\varepsilon_i := \delta \cdot 2^{-(i+1)}$, so $\sum_{i=0}^\infty \varepsilon_i = \delta$. First shift the lower endpoint right to $a' := a + \delta > a$ (for a general Stieltjes function $f$, right-continuity at $a$ chooses $a' > a$ with $f(a') - f(a) < \delta$). Next, since $\ell(S_i) < \infty$, choose $p_i, q'_i \in \mathbb R$ with $S_i \subseteq (p_i, q'_i]$ and $(q'_i - p_i)_+ < \ell(S_i) + \varepsilon_i / 2$, and shift each upper endpoint right to $q_i := q'_i + \varepsilon_i / 2 > q'_i$ (again using right-continuity at $q'_i$ for general $f$). Then $S_i \subseteq (p_i, q'_i] \subseteq (p_i, q_i)$ and $(q_i - p_i)_+ \le (q'_i - p_i)_+ + \varepsilon_i / 2 < \ell(S_i) + \varepsilon_i$. Every $x \in [a', b]$ satisfies $a < a' \le x \le b$, so $[a', b] \subseteq (a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty (p_i, q_i)$. Lemma 1(2) gives
$$
(b - a)_+ \le (b - a')_+ + (a' - a)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty (q_i - p_i)_+ + \delta \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(S_i) + \delta + \delta = \sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(S_i) + \varepsilon.
$$
Taking the infimum over covers $(S_i)$ and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ yields $(b - a)_+ \le \mu^*((a, b])$.

(2) Fix $c \in \mathbb R$ and $T \subseteq \mathbb R$. By **(C1)** (`ofFunction_caratheodory`), $(c, \infty) \in \mathcal C(\mu^*)$ follows once $\ell(T \cap (c, \infty)) + \ell(T \setminus (c, \infty)) \le \ell(T)$. If no $(a, b]$ contains $T$, $\ell(T) = \infty$. Otherwise let $T \subseteq (a, b]$. Since $T \cap (c, \infty) \subseteq (\max(a, c), b]$ and $T \setminus (c, \infty) \subseteq (a, \min(b, c)]$, Lemma 1(1) bounds the left-hand side by $(b - \max(a, c))_+ + (\min(b, c) - a)_+$. Across all four order cases—$b \le a$ ($0 + 0 = 0$), $a < b \le c$ ($0 + (b - a) = b - a$), $c \le a < b$ ($(b - a) + 0 = b - a$), and $a < c < b$ ($(b - c) + (c - a) = b - a$)—this sum equals $(b - a)_+$. Taking the infimum over $(a, b] \supseteq T$ proves $(c, \infty) \in \mathcal C(\mu^*)$. Because $\mathcal C(\mu^*)$ is a $\sigma$-algebra (**C1**), it also contains $(-\infty, d] = \mathbb R \setminus (d, \infty)$, $(-\infty, d) = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty (-\infty, d - \frac{1}{n}]$, every open interval $(c, d) = (c, \infty) \cap (-\infty, d)$, every open set $U \subseteq \mathbb R$ (as the union of rational open intervals $(p, q) \subseteq U$, $p, q \in \mathbb Q$), and therefore $\mathcal B(\mathbb R)$.

(3) Write $\mu^*.\mathrm{trim}(S) := \inf\{ \mu^*(B) : S \subseteq B,\ B \in \mathcal B(\mathbb R) \}$. Monotonicity gives $\mu^*(S) \le \mu^*.\mathrm{trim}(S)$. Conversely, if $\mu^*(S) < \infty$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, pick $S \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty T_i$ with $\sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(T_i) < \mu^*(S) + \varepsilon / 2$, and for each $i$ pick $(a_i, b_i] \supseteq T_i$ with $(b_i - a_i)_+ < \ell(T_i) + \varepsilon 2^{-(i+2)}$. Each $(a_i, b_i] = (a_i, \infty) \cap (-\infty, b_i]$ is Borel, so $B := \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty (a_i, b_i] \in \mathcal B(\mathbb R)$ covers $S$, and countable subadditivity with part (1) gives
$$
\mu^*.\mathrm{trim}(S) \le \mu^*(B) \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty \mu^*\bigl((a_i, b_i]\bigr) = \sum_{i=0}^\infty (b_i - a_i)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(T_i) + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < \mu^*(S) + \varepsilon.
$$
Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ proves $\mu^*.\mathrm{trim} = \mu^*$. In Mathlib (`MeasureSpaceDef.lean:115–116`), a `Measure ℝ` consists of an outer measure that is countably additive on disjoint Borel sequences and satisfies `trim ≤ self`; both fields are now discharged directly by `f.outer` without an extra `toMeasure` wrapper (`Stieltjes.lean:517–522`). $\square$ [Pinned `MeasureSpaceDef.lean:101–175`][ch3-rv-mdef]

**Theorem 1 (real Lebesgue interval lengths, atom-freeness, Haar identification and consumer bounds).** [Lean, read], conditional on **(T1)**, **(C1)**, **(U1)** and **(U2)**. Let $\lambda := (\text{`volume`} : \text{`Measure ℝ`})$.
1. *Identification (`volume_eq_stieltjes_id`).* $\lambda$ equals $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} = \text{`StieltjesFunction.id.measure`}$ on all subsets of $\mathbb R$, is left- and right-translation-invariant, and assigns mass $1$ to $[0, 1]$ and $[-1, 0]$.
2. *Atoms, all four intervals and open decompositions (`volume_singleton`, `volume_Ioc`, `volume_Ioo`, `volume_Icc`, `volume_Ico`, `volume_uIoc`, `nullSingletonClass_volume`).* Every singleton $\{x\}$ and every countable subset $C \subseteq \mathbb R$ satisfy $\lambda(\{x\}) = \lambda(C) = 0$. For all $a, b \in \mathbb R$,
$$
\lambda\bigl((a, b]\bigr) = \lambda\bigl((a, b)\bigr) = \lambda\bigl([a, b]\bigr) = \lambda\bigl([a, b)\bigr) = (b - a)_+,
$$
and on unordered intervals $\lambda(\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)) = \lambda(\operatorname{uIoo}(a, b)) = \lambda(\operatorname{uIcc}(a, b)) = \lvert b - a\rvert$. Every pairwise disjoint countable union $U = \bigsqcup_{n \in J} (a_n, b_n)$ ($a_n \le b_n$) satisfies $\lambda(U) = \sum_{n \in J} (b_n - a_n)$, and adjoining any countable set $E$ gives $\lambda(U \cup E) = \lambda(U)$.
3. *Local finiteness and integration consumers (`locallyFinite_volume`, `integral_const`, `norm_integral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae`).* For every $x \in \mathbb R$, $\lambda((x - 1, x + 1)) = 2 < \infty$, so $\lambda$ is locally finite and finite at every filter finer than $\mathcal N(x)$. For any real Banach space $E$, $a, b \in \mathbb R$, $c \in E$, and $f : \mathbb R \to E$:
   - $\int_a^b c\,d\lambda = (b - a)\cdot c$ (using completeness of $E$ when $c \ne 0$ and $a \ne b$);
   - if $\lVert f(x)\rVert \le C$ for $\lambda$-almost every $x \in \operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)$ (using the named set-integral bound `norm_setIntegral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae'` `[Lean]`), then $\bigl\lVert\int_a^b f(x)\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert \le C\lvert b - a\rvert$. [Pinned `Lebesgue/Basic.lean:54–223`][ch3-rv-lebesgue] · [Pinned `IntervalIntegral/Basic.lean:762–839`][ch3-rv-int-basic]

**Proof.** First establish singleton nullity and the four interval formulas for $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$. Fix $x \in \mathbb R$. Since $\{x\} \subseteq (x - \frac{1}{n+1}, x]$ for every $n \in \mathbb N$, monotonicity and Lemma 2(3) give $0 \le \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(\{x\}) \le \frac{1}{n+1}$ for all $n$, hence $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(\{x\}) = 0$. *(For a general Stieltjes function $f$, `measure_singleton` at `Stieltjes.lean:530–566` chooses $u_n \uparrow a$, writes $(u_0, a] = \{a\} \sqcup \bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (u_n, u_{n+1}]$ of total finite measure $f(a) - f(u_0) < \infty$, and cancels the telescopic sum $\sum_{n=0}^\infty (f(u_{n+1}) - f(u_n)) = f(a^-) - f(u_0)$ to obtain the jump formula $\mu_f(\{a\}) = (f(a) - f(a^-))_+$, which vanishes when $f$ is continuous from the left.)* Countable subadditivity then gives $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(C) \le \sum_{c \in C} \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(\{c\}) = 0$ for every countable $C$.

For $[a, b]$: if $a > b$, $[a, b] = \emptyset$ and $(b - a)_+ = 0$; if $a \le b$, the disjoint Borel decomposition $[a, b] = \{a\} \sqcup (a, b]$ gives $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([a, b]) = 0 + (b - a) = (b - a)_+$ (`measure_Icc`). For $(a, b)$: if $b \le a$, $(a, b) = \emptyset$ and $(b - a)_+ = 0$; if $a < b$, $(a, b] = (a, b) \sqcup \{b\}$ gives $b - a = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((a, b)) + 0$, so $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((a, b)) = (b - a)_+$ (`measure_Ioo`; for general $f$, cancelling the finite atom $f(b) - f(b^-) < \infty$ from $f(b) - f(a)$ yields $(f(b^-) - f(a))_+$). Similarly $[a, b) = \{a\} \sqcup (a, b)$ when $a < b$ gives $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([a, b)) = (b - a)_+$ (`measure_Ico`). In particular $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((x - 1, x + 1)) = 2 < \infty$ and $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([x - 1, x + 1]) = 2 < \infty$, so $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ is locally finite (`instIsLocallyFiniteMeasure`, `Stieltjes.lean:727–731`) and $\sigma$-finite via $\mathbb R = \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty (-(n+1), n+1)$.

Now bridge $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ to $\lambda = \text{`volume`}$ (`volume_eq_stieltjes_id`, `Lebesgue/Basic.lean:54–70`). For any $a \in \mathbb R$ and $p, q \in \mathbb Q$, the preimage of $(p, q)$ under $x \mapsto a + x$ is $(p - a, q - a)$, so
$$
\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}\bigl((a + \cdot)^{-1}(p, q)\bigr) = \bigl((q - a) - (p - a)\bigr)_+ = (q - p)_+ = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}\bigl((p, q)\bigr).
$$
Since $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ is locally finite, **(U1)** implies $(a + \cdot)_*\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ for all $a \in \mathbb R$, i.e. `IsAddLeftInvariant λ_id` (and right translation equals left translation on commutative $\mathbb R$). On both branches $K_0 \in \{[0, 1], [-1, 0]\}$ of `parallelepiped (stdOrthonormalBasis ℝ ℝ)` in **(U2)**, $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([0, 1]) = (1 - 0)_+ = 1$ and $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([-1, 0]) = (0 - (-1))_+ = 1$, so $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(K_0) = 1$. Since $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ is $\sigma$-finite and left-invariant, **(U2)** (`addHaarMeasure_unique`) yields
$$
\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(K_0)\cdot\operatorname{addHaarMeasure}(K_0) = 1\cdot\lambda = \lambda.
$$
Parts (1) and (2) now transfer verbatim to $\lambda$: unordered intervals satisfy $\max(a, b) - \min(a, b) = \lvert b - a\rvert$, pairwise disjoint open intervals sum by **(C1)** on $\mathcal B(\mathbb R)$, and $\lambda(U \cup E) = \lambda(U) + \lambda(E \setminus U) = \lambda(U)$ since $E \setminus U \subseteq E$ is null.

For part (3), $(x - 1, x + 1) \in \mathcal N(x)$ has measure $2 < \infty$, so every filter $\ell' \le \mathcal N(x)$ contains $(x - 1, x + 1)$ (`FTCFilter.finiteAt_inner`). In a complete space $E$, `setIntegral_const` gives $\int_S c\,d\lambda = \lambda.\mathrm{real}(S)\cdot c$ whenever $\lambda(S) < \infty$, where $\lambda.\mathrm{real}(S) := (\lambda(S)).\mathrm{toReal}$. Evaluating on $(a, b]$ and $(b, a]$ and applying the real identity $(t)_+ - (-t)_+ = t$ at $t = b - a$ (`max_zero_sub_eq_self`, `IntervalIntegral/Basic.lean:837–838`) gives
$$
\int_a^b c\,d\lambda = \bigl((b - a)_+ - (a - b)_+\bigr)\cdot c = (b - a)\cdot c.
$$
Finally, $\bigl\lVert\int_a^b f\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert = \bigl\lVert\int_{\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)} f\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert$ (`norm_integral_eq_norm_integral_uIoc`). Since $\lambda(\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)) = \lvert b - a\rvert < \infty$ and $\lambda.\mathrm{real}(\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)) = \lvert b - a\rvert$, `norm_setIntegral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae'` (`Bochner/Set.lean:594–615`) yields $\bigl\lVert\int_a^b f\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert \le C\lvert b - a\rvert$. $\square$ [Pinned `Bochner/Set.lean:588–728`][ch3-rv-set]

**What breaks—and what survives—upon adding a Dirac atom.** [Expository] To isolate the exact role of atom-freeness (`NullSingletonClass`, i.e. $\mu(\{x\}) = 0$ for all $x$), stay on the **same measurable space** $(\mathbb R, \mathcal B(\mathbb R))$ and set
$$
\mu := \lambda + 2\,\delta_{1/2},
$$
which is the Stieltjes measure of the monotone right-continuous jump function $F(x) = x + 2\,\mathbf 1_{[1/2, \infty)}(x)$ (`StieltjesFunction.measure_add`, `Measure/Dirac.lean:74`).
- *What survives (and why set length is never negative).* $\mu$ is a locally finite, $\sigma$-finite Borel measure with values in $[0, \infty]$. Every reversed half-open set $(1, 0] = \emptyset$ still has $\mu((1, 0]) = 0 \ge 0$, while $\mu((0, 1]) = 1 + 2 = 3$. Thus $\int_1^0 1\,d\mu = \mu((1, 0]) - \mu((0, 1]) = 0 - 3 = -3$: the negative sign belongs entirely to the oriented integral difference, not to any negative measure of $(1, 0]$. Moreover, adjacent-interval additivity $\int_a^b f\,d\mu + \int_b^c f\,d\mu = \int_a^c f\,d\mu$ (`integral_add_adjacent_intervals`, `IntervalIntegral/Basic.lean:1095–1098`) holds with no atom-freeness hypothesis on $\mu$ when both `IntervalIntegrable f μ a b` and `IntervalIntegrable f μ b c` hold. For the constant function here both pieces have finite mass, and $(0, 1] = (0, \frac{1}{2}] \sqcup (\frac{1}{2}, 1]$ places the seam point $\frac{1}{2}$ in the left piece once and in the right piece not at all: $\int_0^{1/2} 1\,d\mu + \int_{1/2}^1 1\,d\mu = \frac{5}{2} + \frac{1}{2} = 3 = \int_0^1 1\,d\mu$.
- *What fails without atom-freeness.* At $x_0 = \frac{1}{2}$, $\mu(\{\frac{1}{2}\}) = F(\frac{1}{2}) - F(\frac{1}{2}^-) = \frac{5}{2} - \frac{1}{2} = 2 \ne 0$. Consequently:
  1. *Open, half-open and closed intervals split apart:* $\mu((0, \frac{1}{2})) = \mu([0, \frac{1}{2})) = \frac{1}{2}$ whereas $\mu((0, \frac{1}{2}]) = \mu([0, \frac{1}{2}]) = \frac{5}{2}$; symmetrically, $\mu((\frac{1}{2}, 1)) = \mu((\frac{1}{2}, 1]) = \frac{1}{2}$ whereas $\mu([\frac{1}{2}, 1)) = \mu([\frac{1}{2}, 1]) = \frac{5}{2}$. In Hand calculation 2, since $a_1 = \frac{1}{2} \in E = \overline U \setminus U$, $\mu(U) = \frac{1}{2} \ne \frac{5}{2} = \mu(\overline U)$.
  2. *Endpoint-conversion and single-point modification lemmas fail:* $\int_{[1/2, 1]} 1\,d\mu = \frac{5}{2} \ne \frac{1}{2} = \int_{(1/2, 1]} 1\,d\mu$, breaking `integral_Icc_eq_integral_Ioc` (`Bochner/Set.lean:706–707`, the step used at `WindowHypOfRpow.lean:52`), and the spike $f(\frac{1}{2}) = (10, -7)$ in Hand calculation 1 shifts $\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\mu$ by $2\bigl((10, -7) - (2, -1)\bigr) = (16, -12) \ne (0, 0)$.
  3. *Translation invariance, $(b - a)\cdot c$, $C\lvert b - a\rvert$ and ordinary left-side FTC fail:* $\mu((0, \frac{1}{2})) = \frac{1}{2} \ne \frac{5}{2} = \mu((\frac{1}{4}, \frac{3}{4}))$; $\int_0^1 c\,d\mu = 3c \ne (1 - 0)c$; $\bigl\lvert\int_0^1 1\,d\mu\bigr\rvert = 3 > 1\cdot\lvert 1 - 0\rvert$; and for $G(x) := \int_0^x 1\,d\mu$, right increments at $\frac{1}{2}$ satisfy $G(\frac{1}{2} + h) - G(\frac{1}{2}) = \mu((\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2} + h]) = h$ ($h > 0$), whereas left increments satisfy $G(\frac{1}{2} + h) - G(\frac{1}{2}) = -\mu((\frac{1}{2} + h, \frac{1}{2}]) = h - 2 \to -2 \ne 0$ as $h \uparrow 0$ because $(\frac{1}{2} + h, \frac{1}{2}]$ captures the atom at $\frac{1}{2}$. *(If one instead keeps monotonicity and drops right-continuity, setting $F_{\mathrm{left}}(x) := x + 2\,\mathbf 1_{(1/2, \infty)}(x)$, the raw differences $F_{\mathrm{left}}(\frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{n+1}) - F_{\mathrm{left}}(\frac{1}{2}) = 2 + \frac{1}{n+1} \to 2 \ne 0$ fail downward countable additivity along $(\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{n+1}] \downarrow \emptyset$.)* [Pinned `Measure/Dirac.lean:67–80`][ch3-rv-dirac]

**Remarks on classical literature, attribution and verification scope.**

1. *All-subset envelope versus semiring premeasures.* [Paper] Henri Lebesgue introduced linear outer measure from countable open-interval coverings in his 1902 thesis *Intégrale, longueur, aire* (*Annali di Matematica Pura ed Applicata* (3) **7**, 1902, pp. 231–359), and Constantin Carathéodory formulated outer measures on all subsets and the symmetric cut criterion without inner measure in *Über das lineare Maß von Punktmengen — eine Verallgemeinerung des Längenbegriffs* (*Nachr. Ges. Wiss. Göttingen, Math.-Phys. Kl.*, 1914, pp. 404–426) [Carathéodory (1914)][ch3-rv-cara-paper]. Patrick Billingsley, *Probability and Measure* (3rd ed., Wiley, 1995), proves finite peeling and Heine–Borel subadditivity on $(a, b]$ in Theorem 1.3 (pp. 12–13), extends semiring premeasures via Carathéodory's criterion in Theorems 11.1 and 11.3 and Example 11.3 (pp. 165–168), and constructs Stieltjes measures $\mu((a, b]) = F(b) - F(a)$ for nondecreasing right-continuous $F : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$ (specializing to $F(x) = x$ on p. 176) in Theorem 12.4 and the proof of Theorem 12.5 (pp. 175–179), using the exact two right-continuity shifts and Heine–Borel step on p. 179; see also Terence Tao, [245A Notes 1](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2010/09/09/245a-notes-1-lebesgue-measure/), Lemmas 6–7 for box outer-measure recovery and Exercise 17 for the Carathéodory criterion. Whereas Billingsley first restricts to the semiring of finite intervals and checks premeasure additivity there, Mathlib's `StieltjesFunction` defines $\ell$ directly on all subsets, checks Carathéodory's inequality only on open rays $(c, \infty)$, proves `outer_trim`, and identifies `StieltjesFunction.id.measure` with the inner-product Haar `volume` by `addHaarMeasure_unique`. Because both the classical theorems and pinned Mathlib cover the mathematical result in full, no mathematical priority is claimed here.
2. *Credits and verification boundary.* File-header authors for the pinned Stieltjes, real-Borel, basis-Haar and Lebesgue files are Johannes Hölzl, Sébastien Gouëzel, Yury Kudryashov, Kexing Ying and Mario Carneiro, at Mathlib `0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474` inside Archive A2 (`9631b4f020199e580ba86178cb56c6e9b09f690c2369a80dc4334aa93f49c7fc`). The declarations in Lemma 1, Lemma 2 and Theorem 1 are at author-level local R3 `[Lean, read]` modulo the stated inputs **(T1)**, **(C1)**, **(U1)**, **(U2)** and `norm_setIntegral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae'`; filter pullback, endpoint FTC, Bochner $L^1$ extension, simple-function density, Chapter 15's peak concentration and Chapter 17's radial Haar/Gamma retain their separate scopes. Read-only source inspection is distinct from whole-archive Lean kernel replay, `Main.lean` proof-term reachability, and peer review.
